# Session log

Newest first. One entry per topic, recording what was built, every number that was
measured, and what was verified against which paper section. This is the detailed
build record; [PROGRESS.md](PROGRESS.md) has the status tables and
[PLAN.md](PLAN.md) has the curriculum.

Every performance figure quoted below is reproducible with `./verify.sh` (see
[README.md](README.md)); timings depend on hardware, everything else is seeded.

## 2026-10-27 — topic 57 — Sorting at Scale: Normalized Keys, Radix Sort & Collation

New topic. Read in full: Kuiper & Mühleisen (ICDE 2023), IPS⁴o (arXiv 1705.02257v2), UTS #10 v18.0.0 rev. 55 (§1, §3.6, §7.3, §9.1). Not obtained: Graefe's CSUR survey (ACM paywall; unpaywall reports no OA copy) — its four radix objections are quoted via the ICDE paper §VI-C and the guides say so. Code read at the pins: DuckDB `d54cbbe3df` (`create_sort_key.cpp`, `tuple_data_layout.cpp`, `sort_key.hpp`, `sorted_run.cpp`, `ska_sort.hpp`, `vergesort.h`, `icu_extension.cpp`, `collation_binding.cpp`), FalkorDB `ccb449a9a` (`op_sort.c`), Rust std from rustc 1.99.0's `rust-src`, and two new clones (`ips4o/ips4o` at `08a5b92`, `Voultapher/sort-research-rs` at `8d3eae4`, the latter only cited by the source comments). The pinned DuckDB differs from the paper: vergesort + a ska_sort radix over at most 8 key bytes + full-key comparison for ties, strings encoded as `byte+1` and a `0` delimiter, key width derived from statistics. The guides say where.

**Lane 1 (provided).** Seed 57. 10M `Row64`: `sort_unstable_by` 152.3 ms (0.65 ns/(n·log₂n)), bare `u64` 112.4, stable `sort_by` 211.9; checksum `0812712ec852b87e`. 5M `(int, string)`: b1 (1,000 `a`-values) 545.1 ms, b2 (`a` over `i32`) 111.3, b3 (20-byte shared prefix) 530.0; checksums `534fdd4bcaf33e4b` / `1cd6d972296e3024` / `c23c7c8734b16de6`; ties on `a` 4,999,000 / 2,904 / 4,999,000. 2M collation strings: 13.6 bytes average, 1,978,777 distinct, collating comparator 744.8 ms (17.79 ns/(n·log₂n)). Counts identical in four runs; second run (load 4.83) within 4%.

**Reference numbers (scratch solutions, 22 tests green, not committed).** `u64` LSD/MSD speed-up over the comparator: 2.06×/1.62× at 10M, 2.22×/1.78× at 50M, 0.19×/0.84× at 100 rows, 1.06×/0.68× at 10,000; presorted 0.04×/0.02×, reverse 0.08×/0.04×, 256 distinct 1.42×/0.54×, 3 live bytes 3.20×/1.04×. `(int, string)` radix: b1 3.00×, b2 1.08×, b3 0.71×; shared-prefix sweep 0/4/8/12/16/24/40 bytes → 1.93/2.36/1.79/0.69/0.71/0.72/0.73×. Lane 3 sort keys vs collating comparator, n = 16…2,000,000: 1.04×…1.76× (second run 1.00×…1.72×), 8.6…43.2 counted comparisons per string, 82 key bytes per string, g 34–49 ns, c 9–18 ns, b 3–8 ns, m* 8.2–13.8; primary-level classes 490,966 vs 490,842 predicted. IPS⁴o (C++, sequential) vs `std::sort`, 16-byte rows: 2.07× at 1M, 2.34× at 10M. Negative results: Graefe's "worse cache efficiency" does not reproduce up to 50M rows; the paper's "LSD for ≤ 4-byte keys, MSD above" does not transfer to 8-byte keys with the skip optimisation on this machine (caveat: the reference MSD is out-of-place with copy-back).

**Lessons.** (1) The first lane-1c comparator validated UTF-8 on every string access and measured 2,395–2,984 ms; removing the validation (validate once in `StrSet::new`) gave 745 ms and changed lane 3's speed-up from ~5× to ~1.7× — a hidden per-call cost in the *baseline* inflates a technique's win. (2) Wall-clock timings at load 15–40 were unusable (a 1M sort slower than a 50M one); thread CPU time made them reproducible within 5% at load ~5. (3) The Rust base case differs by element size (sorting network for ≤ 8-byte `Copy` types, general small sort at 16 bytes), so a bare-`u64` sort is not the same algorithm as a `Row64` sort.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 56 — Memory Allocators for Database Workloads

**New topic 56, `Memory Allocators for Database Workloads`** — six reading guides (mimalloc, jemalloc, Durner–Leis–Neumann, Hoard, Mesh, and FalkorDB/Redis's allocator path), a graph-engine allocation bench with the system allocator, a bump arena and feature-gated mimalloc/jemalloc comparison, and two stub lanes (a page-sharded size-class allocator; cross-thread frees without blowup).

**Lane 1** (seeded, `trace.rs` seed 56: 200,000 nodes → **1,797,141** objects, **80,949,568 B = 77.2 MiB** requested, **997,374** 40-byte edge records; every count reproduces exactly; each phase in a fresh child process, best-of-3 time, median-of-3 memory; Apple M3 Pro, load average 4.72 at the start). 1a bulk load: libmalloc 14.0 ns/alloc, resident 86.3 MiB (**1.12×** live); arena 4.8 ns, 77.6 MiB (1.00×). 1b churn (3 rounds, free a random half, insert replacements with grown properties; live after 132.1 MiB): libmalloc 29.6 ns/op, **216.2 MiB (1.64×)**; arena 15.6 ns, 288.6 MiB (2.18×, never reclaims). 1c query temporaries (400 × 20,000 objects): libmalloc **26.4 ns** per allocate+free, arena **1.7 ns** (15.5×). 1d cross-thread (4 × 1,000,000): libmalloc 33.1 ns same-thread, **46.5 ns** other-thread (1.40×).

**Allocator comparison** (`--features mimalloc` = crate 0.1.52, mimalloc 2.x C source; `--features jemalloc` = tikv-jemallocator 0.6.1, jemalloc 5.3.0): 1a 8.2 / 10.1 ns (res/live 1.12 / 1.13); 1b 19.2 / 23.7 ns, 1.15× / 1.15×; 1c 9.7 / 16.1 ns; 1d same-thread 9.2 / 13.6 ns, other-thread 21.1 / 15.4 ns. A second run moved single cells by up to 30% and no ranking. **The quantum:** `JEMALLOC_SYS_WITH_LG_QUANTUM=3` (Redis's `--with-lg-quantum=3`, `deps/Makefile:162`) takes the bulk load from 87.0 to **78.2 MiB (1.13× → 1.01×, −10.1%) and churn from 1.15× to 1.08×, because the 40-byte edge stops rounding to 48 (997,374 × 8 B = 7.6 MiB).

**Negative / unexplained.** libmalloc's post-churn residency is bimodal: one binary 1.64× in 20 of 20 fresh processes, another 1.13–1.14× in 15 of 20 and 1.64× in 5 (and in 12 more runs, with and without `MallocNanoZone=0`, 1.64× in 12 of 12 and 6/6 split); `footprint` equals `resident`, so it is not reusable pages. Cause not found — exercise 3. A toy page-sharded allocator (reference, uncommitted) matches jemalloc on this trace (21.0 vs 23.7 ns/op, 1.07× vs 1.15×) — a statement about page-sharded free lists on this trace, not about jemalloc.

**Reference lanes** (scratch crate, not committed): lane 2 `PageHeap` 8.5 ns bulk (1.01×), 21.0 ns/op churn (**1.07×**), 7.2 ns/obj query temporaries; lane 3 `SharedHeap` **15.5 ns/obj** cross-thread (libmalloc 46.5), 42–43 pages (40 once) vs ~440 without remote-free reuse. All 20 tests pass against the reference (10 provided, 6 lane-2, 4 lane-3); the committed crate fails exactly the 10 stub tests.

**Papers and code, each checked against the text.** mimalloc TR (§1 "over 25%" Lean, §2.2 bump pointer "about 2% worse", §3.4 `gcc` 30% / "over 18000" pages, `xmalloc-test` 30%, §4 redis 14% over jemalloc); Durner et al. (abstract 2.7×; Tables 1–2 transcribed, inflation ratios computed: 2.23 / 2.10 / 1.76 / 3.02 / 1.58); Hoard (§1 blowup definition, §2.2, §3 invariant, §4.1 O(1), §5 parameters S = 8 KiB, f = 1/4, K = 4); Mesh (§1 Robson 13×, §2.2 10⁻¹⁵² = 256⁻⁶³ checked, §6.2.1 632 → 530 MB = 16.1%, §6.2.2 0.23 s vs 1.49 s — the paper's printed "5.5×" disagrees with its own seconds, 6.5×; meshing probabilities `C(256−k,k)/C(256,k)` computed here, not from the paper). Evans' BSDCan 2006 jemalloc paper and TCMalloc's OSDI'21 paper were not obtained and are not cited. Source anchors: FalkorDB `ccb449a9a` and redis `e87dda2a4` through `pinned-source.py`; mimalloc `8b08dbb8f9c8` (`main3`) and jemalloc `0697b617fbaa` (`dev`) read from new clones (not yet pinned). Found while reading: FalkorDB's `rm_aligned_malloc` ignores its alignment; `n_alloced` adds requested and subtracts usable bytes (the file's own comment says so); Redis builds jemalloc with an 8-byte quantum, the crate default is 16.

**Gates.** `check-reading-depth.py --check topics/56-memory-allocators/` 6/6; `check-mermaid.js` 6 blocks, 0 errors; relative links in the topic resolve (a full `mdbook build` needs the SUMMARY entries above); `cargo build --release --all-targets` with `-D warnings` clean, also with `--features mimalloc`.

(fix typo at integration: the `−10.1%)]` should read `−10.1%)`.)

## 2026-10-26 — topic 55 — Datalog Engines: Recursion as Joins and as Sparse Algebra

**What was built.** `topics/55-datalog-engines/`: README, six reading guides (Soufflé; RecStep; Ascent; CozoDB; the 2026 GPU and distributed worst-case-optimal Datalog preprints; Datalog as linear algebra), `notes.md`, and a dependency-light Rust crate `datalog-experiments` (committed `Cargo.lock`) with lane 1 implemented and lanes 2 and 3 stubbed; `check-reading-depth.py --check` reads 6/6, every mermaid block parses, no internal link is broken.

**Lane 1 (provided), Apple M3 Pro, load average 13.51 at the start; counts exact and identical across two runs.** Linear TC naive / semi-naive derivations, rounds, new, duplicates left: power-law n=1500 (3,489 arcs) 2,109,908 / 137,482 (15.3×), 21 rounds, 65,289 new, 72,193 duplicates; grid 24×24 (1,104) 5,248,416 / 165,600 (31.7×), 46 rounds, 89,424, 76,176; chain 500 (499) 41,417,499 / 124,750 (332×), 499 rounds, 124,750, 0; 6,000 chains × 10 (54,000) 1,494,000 / 270,000 (5.5×), 9 rounds, 270,000, 0. Same generation: power-law n=600 (960) 278,931 / 64,787 (4.3×), 25,408 new, 39,379 duplicates; grid 619,986 / 35,468 (17.5×), 9,177, 26,291. Nonlinear TC naive / textbook / old-delta: power-law 5,882,220 / 2,749,550 / 2,143,923 (6 rounds; old-delta 22.0% fewer than textbook), grid 17,567,264 / 8,114,570 / 6,581,680 (7; 18.9%), chain 45,138,521 / 24,420,281 / 20,708,999 (10; 15.2%) — 15.6×, 39.7×, 166× the linear semi-naive derivations. Goal-directed `?- tc(0,y)`: 1,808 / 1,104 / 499 / 9 derivations (661 / 575 / 499 / 9 tuples) against 137,482 / 165,600 / 124,750 / 270,000. Timings in the README (best of 3, quoted run): linear naive / semi-naive 107.05 / 13.01 ms power-law, 1550.63 / 16.84 ms chain; nonlinear old-delta 95.76 ms power-law, 1638.68 ms chain; sg 15.62 / 3.33 ms.

**Reference solution for lanes 2 and 3 (scratch copy, not committed; load average 5.81).** Lane 2, derivations Left / Right / Adaptive on the power-law graph 137,482 / 124,357 / 119,159 (Adaptive = the BFS-level oracle |arc| + Σ_r min(L_r, R_r); 13.3% under Left, 4.2% under Right), probes 169,244 / 161,822 / 160,296, 4.59 / 4.38 / 4.39 ms against hash semi-naive 13.21 ms; grid, chain and 10-chains tie at 165,600 / 124,750 / 270,000; sorted 4.4–6.0 ms on the grid (hash 15.09), 38.0–38.7 ms on the chain (hash 15.91, sorted 2.4× slower — a full-closure rewrite per round), 4.4–4.8 ms on the 10-chains (hash 5.98). Lane 3 flops / new entries / ms (lane-1 hash ms): power-law 133,993 / 61,800 / 0.80 (13.21), grid 164,496 / 88,320 / 0.46 (15.09), chain 124,251 / 124,251 / 1.45 (15.91), 10-chains 216,000 / 216,000 / 7.57 (5.98, algebra 1.27× slower: n = 60,000 → 450 MB mask), sg power-law 85,124 / 25,408 / 0.41 (2.82) against lane 1's 64,787 derivations, sg grid 54,328 / 9,177 / 0.27 (0.38); flops + |arc| equals the semi-naive derivation count on all four closure graphs. Per-source bit-matrix (RecStep PBME): 1.83 / 1.48 / 2.87 / 24.45 ms, derivations equal to semi-naive's.

**Negative result recorded.** A static `Left`/`Right` choice cannot come from degree or walk-count statistics: Σ_z out(z)·(walks ending at z) = Σ_z in(z)·(walks starting at z) for every length, so such an estimator always ties and picks `Left` — right on 21 of 40 seeded power-law graphs (`power_law(300, 900, seed)`, seeds 0–39), worst ratio 1.535. Sampled-BFS estimators were right on 16 / 18 / 26 / 24 / 28 of 40 at 8 / 16 / 32 / 64 / 128 sources and 40 of 40 at all 300; hence the per-round `Adaptive` lane. Cozo's rule-level semi-naive makes nonlinear TC cost the naive row (2.74× the disjoint split on the power-law graph).

**Paper audit.** SRDatalog (arXiv 2604.20073v2) Table 2, 17 rows: geometric mean of raw time ratios vs Ascent 17.6× (text 21×), vs FlowLog 13.4× (14×), vs Soufflé 26.4× (26×); the abstract's 21×–47× is not in the table; the clean GPU-vs-GPU rows are 2.3×–7.1× (VFLog, SG) and 2.1×–4.0× (cuMatch, LSQB). Distributed paper (arXiv 2610.12692v1): Table I edges 66 / 17 / 12 × N_E for 1D / 2D / 3D; 2D out of memory at 32 ranks on Polaris for the two Orkut workloads; Table V loses to FlowLog on Andersen Large (17.519 vs 9.408 s) and to Soufflé and FlowLog on SG (78.235 vs 57.347 and 31.905 s); Table VI's 8.9×–11.2× over Slog is TC (a two-atom join). RecStep (arXiv 1812.03975): OOF 63% / 41% / 24% on CSPA/httpd (Figure 2). Sato (arXiv 1608.00139): Matrix 0.094–0.117 s at every p_e for N = 1,000, slower than Prolog and ASP at p_e = 0.001 and 15–10⁴× faster from 0.01 (Table 2).

**Anchors.** Three new clones (below); 62 `file:line` anchors into them checked by script against the files (content at the cited line); the two GraphBLAS/LAGraph anchors checked with `pinned-source.py`. The Soufflé CAV'16 / PPoPP'19 / PMAM'19 PDFs could not be fetched (the host was blocked by the sandbox), so nothing is attributed to them; the Soufflé chapter rests on the PVLDB'18 paper and the source. FlowLog (VLDB 2026) is covered in topic 27's README and only cross-linked.

## 2026-10-27 — topic 54 — Main-Memory Join Algorithms: Hash, Radix-Partitioned & Sort-Merge

**New topic 54, `Main-Memory Join Algorithms`** — the three designs (one shared table, radix-partitioned, sort-merge) on one seeded workload (R with 2¹⁴–2²⁴ unique-key 8-byte tuples, S with 2²⁵ foreign-key probes), five reading guides (Balkesen et al. PVLDB'13, Schuh et al. SIGMOD'16, Bandle et al. SIGMOD'21, DuckDB v2.0.0's join table, DataFusion `350ca7650`'s hash join), a Rust crate with lane 1 provided and lanes 2–3 stubbed (12 failing tests as the specification), and a reference solution (uncommitted) that fills notes.md's reference tables.

**Lane 0 measured the machine rather than quoting it** (Apple M3 Pro; `sysctl`: performance-core L1d 128 KiB, cache line 128 B, page 16 KiB, performance-cluster L2 16 MiB, efficiency L2 4 MiB; no system-level-cache size is exposed): dependent-load latency 0.99–1.07 ns up to 128 KiB, 5.25 ns at 512 KiB, 6.04 at 2 MiB, 8.31 at 8 MiB, 45 at 16 MiB (19–45 between runs), 91.8 at 32 MiB, 112.7 at 1 GiB; by pages (one node per 16 KiB page) 1.06 ns at 128 pages, 2.65 at 256, 4.22 at 1,024, 6.56 at 2,048, 15.16 at 4,096, 23.87 at 65,536; read bandwidth 70.0 GB/s with one thread, 117.2 with eleven.

**Lane 1** (|S| = 33,554,432, thread CPU time, best of 3, load average 4.71): build 0.08 / 0.45 / 2.09 / 9.35 / 56.11 / 241.51 ms for 2¹⁴…2²⁴; batched probe 284.87 / 294.10 / 301.39 / 356.80 / 663.80 / 719.42 ms = 8.49 / 8.76 / 8.98 / 10.63 / 19.78 / 21.44 ns per probe; the one-slot-per-step probe 464.16 / 504.93 / 535.40 / 623.78 / 1693.13 / 2307.70 ms. Lane 1a (tables in L1/L2, |S| = 2²⁴): scalar / bucket / batched 8.59 / 6.02 / 6.46 ns at 2⁴, 11.77 / 7.52 / 8.11 at 2⁸, 13.74 / 8.61 / 8.43 at 2¹⁴. Lane 1d (random vs sorted-by-home-bucket probes): 8.70 vs 4.38 ns (2¹⁴, 1.99×), 12.17 vs 5.00 (2²⁰, 2.43×), 27.66 vs 5.10 (2²², 5.42×), 32.66 vs 5.80 (2²⁴, 5.63×). Lane 1b (2²⁴): Zipf 0.75 / 0.99 probes 0.91× / 0.68× of uniform (1118.17 ms). Lane 1c: 703.34 / 379.61 / 193.54 / 127.26 ms on 1 / 2 / 5 / 11 threads (1.85 / 3.63 / 5.53×). Checksum of the lane-2 oracle `a1f3afb6193b238f`, identical across runs.

**Reference solution** (|S| = 2²⁵, ms; NPJ / best radix (bits) / LSD sort-merge): 2¹⁴ 288.8 / 221.3 (12) / 250.2; 2¹⁶ 303.7 / 248.9 (12) / 248.4; 2¹⁸ 307.3 / 264.8 (9) / 268.8; 2²⁰ 431.7 / 277.5 (10) / 258.6; 2²² 719.1 / 311.2 (10) / 358.3; 2²⁴ 973.5 / 424.9 (12) / 544.1. Partition phase 54.5–63.1 ms up to 2²² (97.1 at 2²⁴) = 1.6–1.8 ns per tuple against a 7.9 ms read+write floor at 70 GB/s; two passes lose 16–20% (2²⁰ 323.2 vs 277.5; 2²² 371.8 vs 311.2; 2²⁴ 504.6 vs 424.9), write-combine buffers lose 7–11% (2²⁰ 297.9 vs 277.5; 2²⁴ 471.0 vs 424.9); at 2²⁴ 4 bits (Equation (1) with the 16 MiB L2) is the worst plain single-pass point, 933.1 ms, and 12 bits the best, 424.9. At |R| = 2²⁴: 10% hits NPJ 863.9 vs radix 405.6 ms, Zipf 0.99 774.8 vs 402.1. Tiny builds (2⁴…2¹²): NPJ 213.8–267.6 ms vs radix 183–201.

**The negative result.** Bandle et al.'s "partitioning rarely pays when the build side fits the cache" does not reproduce on 8-byte tuples: the radix join wins at every size, and lane 1d shows why below the cache cliff — the shared table's probe loop costs 6–8 ns even for a 16-tuple table, and sorted probe order halves it, so what partitioning buys at small sizes is order, not fewer misses. The case where the shared table should win (32–64-byte payloads, a selective join without a pre-filter) is not constructed by the reference; Exercises 4–5 build it. Equation (1) holds only with the L1d as the cache (11 bits predicted at 2²⁴, 12 measured).

**Process notes.** The first probe loop (slot at a time) and a first timing harness (wall time under load average 17–31, threads landing on efficiency cores) gave numbers that were discarded: single-threaded lanes now use thread CPU time (`timing.rs`) and print the dependent-add period of the core they ran on; the bucket probe loop was added when lane 1a showed a 9–12 ns floor on an all-L1 table. The probe loop's remaining floor was not decomposed (Exercise 3). A scratch experiment (not in the repo) measured 1.2 ns for a straight-line 4-load probe with no exit branch; it is not cited as a result. All five guides pass `tools/check-reading-depth.py --check`; the README's mermaid diagram parses.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 53 — Cardinality Estimation for Graph Patterns: Where Multi-Hop Estimation Dies

Motivated by topic 13's open question ("where does multi-hop estimation die", `notes.md:122`, `reading-ldbc-snb.md:421`) and topic 10's relational audit. Papers read from the PDF: G-CARE (SIGMOD'20; ntu.edu.sg copy), Pessimistic Cardinality Estimation (arXiv 2412.00642), LpBound (arXiv 2502.05912), GOpt (arXiv 2401.17786), Color (arXiv 2405.06767v2). Not obtained: Neumann & Moerkotte, "Characteristic Sets" (ICDE'11 — the Mannheim and TUM copies returned HTML; the estimator is quoted through G-CARE §3.2 and a Waterloo slide deck was not used). Not read: GLogS (ATC'23), "Still Asking" (VLDB'25), Kayali & Suciu's quasi-stable coloring, Markl et al.'s progressive optimization.

**Lane 1 (provided).** Seeded labelled Zipf graph (`Params::lane1(seed, pop)`, seeds 1–5): 27,000 nodes in 4 labels [3000, 6000, 12000, 6000]; 5 edge types r0 L0>L1, r1 L1>L2, r2 L2>L3, r3 L3>L0, r4 L2>L0; edge draws [40k, 60k, 90k, 60k, 30k] de-duplicated (seed 1: [34374, 53343, 80401, 51749, 28132] = 247,999 edges); Zipf 0.6 sources / 1.0 destinations; `Shared` popularity (one ranking per label) vs `Independent` (a ranking per type and role). Patterns: all label-consistent walks of 2 / 3 / 4 edges (6 / 7 / 9 per graph; 30 / 35 / 45 over five seeds) plus triangle r0>r1>r4. Independence (Kùzu `getExtensionRate` = Neo4j node-connection product), median / p90 / max q (under): shared — hop 2 9.6 / 13.8 / 15.0 (100%), hop 3 57.5 / 65.1 / 67.0 (100%), hop 4 324.0 / 372.6 / 394.1 (100%); independent — 1.1 / 1.2 / 1.8 (43%), 1.1 / 1.7 / 2.0 (49%), 1.1 / 1.7 / 1.9 (58%). Memgraph constant `|L|·3^k`: shared 62.2 / 822.1 / 12,425.7 medians, independent 6.4 / 18.6 / 40.1. Max-degree bound: shared 4.4 / 40.8 / 294.4, independent 40.8 / 1,741.3 / 83,367.0. Two-hop degree-sequence bound: 1.0 shared, 9.7 independent; ℓ2 bound 1.3 and 12.4. Seed-1 detail: r0>r1 true 3,009,074, estimate 305,602.0 (q 9.8); r0>r1>r2 124,840,475 vs 2,047,559.2 (61.0); r0>r1>r2>r3 6,150,191,151 vs 17,659,856.7 (348.3). Triangles r0>r1>r4: shared seeds 1–3 true 54,738 / 56,124 / 54,948, independence ≈ 238.8 (q 229.2 / 235.0 / 230.3), AGM 7,182,143 (131.2×), ℓ2 712,488 (13.0×); independent true 234 / 234 / 404, independence q 1.0 / 1.0 / 1.7, AGM q 30,678.4 / 30,653.2 / 17,729.4, ℓ2 q 3,019.4 / 3,027.8 / 1,723.4. Plan choice (2^(k−1) left-deep orders, cost = rows materialised): independence picks a non-optimal order for 5 / 35 (3 hops) and 5 / 45 (4 hops) on the shared graph with geomean regret 1.00, worst < 1.005, answer share of the best order's cost 97.7% / 98.3%; independent graph 4 / 35 and 4 / 45 wrong, worst regret 1.06 / 1.03, answer share 89.5% / 91.0%. Sanity: 2-hop DP = Σ in·out for every 2-walk; forward DP = backward DP on every 4-walk; single edge exact; max-degree bound never below truth. Two runs byte-identical. Nothing timed.

**Negative result.** A 348× underestimate did not change any plan's cost: the answer is 90–98% of the cheapest order's cost and every order pays it (G-CARE §6.5 reports the same for star-shaped queries). Exercise 7 constructs a selective-predicate case where it should matter; not measured.

**Reference solutions (scratch; not committed).** Lane 2 characteristic sets, shared popularity, seeds 1–5: median / p90 / max q 8.7 / 10.3 / 10.8 (hop 2), 41.6 / 43.9 / 44.7 (3), 204.3 / 231.6 / 236.9 (4), all underestimates; 21 sets on seed 1 — a 1.1× / 1.4× / 1.6× gain over independence; negative result: a set records which types a node has, not how many. Lane 3 two-edge catalog: 1.0 / 1.0 / 1.0, 2.0 / 2.2 / 2.2, 3.2 / 3.5 / 3.6, never an underestimate, 11 stored patterns (F2[0][1]=3,009,074, [1][2]=4,607,815, [1][4]=1,779,714, [2][3]=6,685,881, [3][0]=4,198,038, [4][0]=2,659,294); seed-1 r0>r1>r2 catalog 259,926,444.2 vs true 124,840,475 (characteristic sets 2,971,857.9); r0>r1>r2>r3 21,614,622,638.0 vs 6,150,191,151 (26,552,817.3). Plan choice with either estimator: 0–5 wrong picks, regret 1.00. The catalog's covariance of in(b), out(c) over middle edges is negative for all seven three-edge patterns on seed 1 (−1,010 … −5,334) and sign(estimate − truth) = sign(−covariance) for all seven. All 28 tests green with the references (16 provided tests pass, 12 stub tests fail on the committed crate).

**Code reading (pinned).** Kùzu: per-hop factor `numRels / atLeastOne(numBoundNodes)` (`cardinality_estimator.cpp:219`), applied as a group multiplier on a factorized Extend (`append_extend.cpp:88-91`), constants 0.01 / 0.1 (`constants.h:159-160`). Neo4j: node cardinalities × per-connection multipliers `E(rel)/(card(left)·card(right))` with an unbound endpoint costing 1 (`NodeConnectionCardinalityModel.scala:148-167`), statistic = counts store of (label)-[type]->(label) (`TransactionBoundGraphStatistics.scala:149`); for a labelled path it reduces to `E_r·E_s/|B|`, identical to Kùzu's. Memgraph: constant `kExpand = 3.0` per Expand unless `SymbolStatistics.degree` is supplied (`cost_estimator.hpp:368-380`, `cost_constants.hpp:47`). FalkorDB (C and Rust): no cardinality estimate — a priority score (bound 3 / filtered 2 / labelled 1, labels counted *on the query node*) with a node-count-per-label tie-break (`traverse_order_utils.c:36-53, 225-308`, `cost_base_label_scan.c:216-220`, `select_scan_node.rs:79-111`); matrices multiplied left to right under `GxB_ANY_PAIR_BOOL` (`algebraic_expression_mul.c:33, 36-58`).

**Paper discrepancies.** Color Example 4: text says average degree "≈ 4" and `10100·4^k`; the stated graphs give 29,900 / 10,100 = 2.96 (guide uses 2.96; exact `100·99^k + 10000·2^k` is 1,020,100 / 97,109,900 / 9,606,120,100 at k = 2 / 3 / 4, traditional 88,516 / 262,042 / 775,748). Color §1 "1–2 orders of magnitude" vs §3 "over 2 orders" for Figure 2. G-CARE's validity range is Markl et al.'s concept (ref. [27]), not Kabra & DeWitt's. LpBound's error metric is estimate ÷ truth, not q-error.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 52 — Graph Reordering & Compression: Bytes per Edge, and Why Vertex IDs Are the Lever

Built to answer a FalkorDB developer's question: how many bytes per edge do
CSR/GraphBLAS matrices cost, and is the vertex-id order — not the codec — the
real lever for size and for cache misses? Papers downloaded and read: Boldi &
Vigna WebGraph I (WWW'04, all tables), Dhulipala et al. (arXiv 1602.08820v1,
§3–§5, Tables 2–3), Shun et al. Ligra+ (DCC'15, §3–§4, Tables 1–2, Figure 5),
Brisaboa et al. k²-trees (SPIRE'09, all), and the ReScience replication of
Gorder (Lécuyer et al., 2021, in full). Not obtained: the SIGMOD'16 Gorder
paper, Rabbit Order (IPDPS'16; the IPDPS URL returned HTML), the k²-tree journal
version (abstract only). New clone: `webgraph-rs` (see below). Code read:
`ligra/byte.h`, `byteRLE.h`; `webgraph-rs` `bvcomp.rs`, `flags.rs`, `algo/src/llp`;
FalkorDB `module.c`, `tensor.c`, `delta_new.c`, `graph.c`; GraphBLAS header.

**Lane 1 (provided).** Seeded community graph (seed 52): n = 6,000,000,
m = 64,747,251 (mean out-degree 10.79, max 1,756), 21,972 planted communities,
75% intra-community edges; loaded with random ids (`shuffle_perm` seed 1).
Per ordering (LogGap / γ / varint / +vertex bits per edge / simulated 4 MiB
8-way miss % / CSR ns per edge / varint ns per edge / BFS ms), run at load 5.27:
random 18.01 / 35.78 / 24.11 / 30.04 / 90.3 / 3.27 / 9.95 / 2541.0; in-degree
sorted 17.74 / 35.35 / 24.00 / 29.93 / 85.9 / 3.06 / 9.24 / 2535.5; BFS 14.68 /
29.68 / 20.98 / 26.91 / 62.0 / 2.71 / 7.95 / 1591.7; planted 8.72 / 18.17 /
14.40 / 20.33 / 23.5 / 2.44 / 5.44 / 1859.1. Order time 87 / 308 / 1,023 ms.
Scan checksum e5358af47cfd3b71; three BFSs reach 17,992,203 vertices, examine
194,160,603 edges, depth sum 26 — identical under every labelling. The bit sizes,
miss rates and BFS counts were identical between two runs; an unlocked run at
load 14.93 gave 3.87 → 2.81 ns/edge CSR, 12.17 → 6.28 varint, 2809 → 2202 ms BFS.
Headline: random → planted ids = 1.67× fewer varint bits, 1.34× faster gather,
1.37× faster BFS; degree sorting 0.5% (negative); BFS order −13% in 1 s; the
varint scan is 2.2× slower than CSR at planted ids and 3.0× at random ids (one
thread — Ligra+'s win is a 40-thread result). At n = 2,000,000 (16 MB of values,
inside the 16 MB P-cluster L2) the orderings did not separate in time (random
1.37 ns/edge, planted 2.63, a locked run) while the simulated misses did (72% vs
18%) — why the lane uses 6 M.

**Lane 1c probes.** `probe/graphblas_bytes.c` against FalkorDB's libgraphblas.a
(v10.5.0), 1 M × 1 M, 7,999,964 entries: GrB_BOOL iso 36,000,108 B = 4.500 B/edge,
iso off 5.500, GrB_UINT64 edge ids 12.500, 32-bit offsets and indices in all.
`GRAPH.MEMORY USAGE` after bulk loading 200,000 nodes + 1 M / 2 M / 4 M edges:
relation_matrices 26 / 49 / 94 MB, total 36 / 68 / 131 MB; slope 23.8 B/edge
(relation matrices), 33.2 (graph). Settled-matrix arithmetic predicts 17–18; the
gap is open (README exercise 6).

**Lanes 2 and 3 (stubs).** Reference (not committed) run at load 5.27: bisection
(leaf 32, 20 iterations, seed 1, top 3 recursion levels on 8 threads) 204,425 ms;
LogGap 8.51 / γ 17.82 / varint 14.52 / +vertex 20.45 / miss 26.0% / CSR 2.59 /
varint 5.97 ns / BFS 1898 ms — within 0.8% of the oracle's bytes, below its
LogGap. Small-graph test LogGap random 9.951, BFS 8.335, bisection 5.788. WebGraph
coding (W 7, R 3, L_min 4): lane-1 graph gaps-only / varint / BV bits per edge —
random 26.11 / 24.11 / 26.30, BFS 22.10 / 20.98 / 21.97, planted 14.41 / 14.40 /
14.56, bisection 14.20 / 14.52 / 14.35; web-like graph (40% copies, m =
65,476,552) random 26.17 / 24.13 / 26.35, planted 14.44 / 14.39 / 10.15. BV
decode-scan 21.98–28.50 ns/edge vs varint 4.63–10.04 vs CSR 2.01–3.28; encode
~10 s. Negative result: references cost 1% on independent lists; on copied lists
they save 29.7%. 23 repo tests: 15 provided pass, 8 stubs fail by design;
`-D warnings` build clean; reference green.

**Discrepancy found.** WebGraph paper: ν(x) = 2|x| − 1 for x < 0 gives ν(−2) = 3,
but the worked residual example and Table 5 print 5; dsi-bitstream's `to_nat`
(−2x − 1) follows the formula.

**Verification.** `tools/check-reading-depth.py --check` 7/7; mermaid 0 errors;
`tools/check-book-links.py` on a scratch build (placeholder topic 51 page): only
the topic-51 link unresolved.

## 2026-10-27 — topic 51 — Dynamic Graph Storage: A CSR That Takes Writes

**New topic 51, `Dynamic Graph Storage: A CSR That Takes Writes`** — FalkorDB's delta matrix (topic 20) priced against LiveGraph, Sortledton, Teseo, Aspen and Packed CSR on one seeded graph, with an independent comparison (Su et al., SIGMOD 2025) and a real C FalkorDB datapoint. Six reading guides (Revisiting, Sortledton, Teseo, Packed CSR, LiveGraph, FalkorDB's delta in context), README, notes.md with reference numbers, a crate with lane 1 provided and lanes 2–3 stubbed (11 stub tests), `scripts/falkordb_datapoint.py`, `dump_edges`.

**Lane 1** (R-MAT scale 20, seed 51; |V| 1,048,576, |E| 16,084,545, max out-degree 39,354, 502,214 isolated, top 1% own 49.8%; Apple M3 Pro, load 4.49): splice insert **0.641 ms** (delete 0.664; 34 MB moved at 53 GB/s); two-hop scan 0.161 / 0.160 / 0.145 ns/edge (CSR / empty delta / `Vec<Vec<u32>>`) over 36,871,342 edges touched; 1% writes: delta 220 ns/write, adjacency list 1,237, splice 615,536; 10% inserts: delta 490 (1 flush, 6.60 ms), adjacency list 555; 10% half deletes: delta scan 0.189, **per-edge tombstone test 0.339 (1.98×)**, adjacency list 0.171; growth ×empty 0.94 / 0.97 / 1.09 / **2.14** at 1,000 / 10,000 / 100,000 / 1,000,000 pending (1,030,138 pending over 188,772 DP rows); flush threshold: T = 1,000 → 18 flushes of 3.11 ms, 2,959 ns/write; T = 10,000 → 1 flush of 6.28 ms, 468 ns/write (154 excluding the flush; steady state 154 + 628 = **782 ns**, 1.4× the adjacency list); bytes/edge 4.26 / 4.43 / 5.56.

**Negative results.** (1) The per-edge tombstone scan was mistaken for "delta growth" until lane 1c's deletion mix disagreed with 1d; splitting rows at tombstones (binary search each) removes it. (2) A stream average hid the flush: 468 ns/insert at T = 10,000 is 782 in steady state. (3) A PMA CSR's scan is **8.3×** a CSR's (1.376 vs 0.165 ns/edge), 5.4× with a branchless gap compaction, against PCSR's "within a factor of two" — the paper's Table II itself shows CSR 1.69–2.78× faster on real graphs, not the "within 25%" of its synthetic figures. (4) Revisiting's text ranges (1.2–53.7×, 2.4–11.0×) do not reproduce from its Table 5, which recomputes to 1.11–4.10×.

**Reference numbers (reference solutions, not committed):** lane 2 (sorted blocks, capacity 256) scan 0.206 ns/edge (1.25×), 7.98 B/edge, writes 588 / 404 / 341 ns; lane 3 (PMA, leaf 16) 1.376 (8.3×), 8.87 B/edge, writes 376 / 359 / 369; branchless PMA 0.867 (5.4×).

**C FalkorDB** (redis-server 8.6.2 + `falkordb.so`, port 6390, shut down): 16,084,545 relations bulk-loaded in 23.0 s; `count(c)` equals the Rust distinct two-hop vertex count for all 200 starts (FalkorDB's traversal is set-valued: 1,837,771 distinct vs 3,742,650 paths); engine 891 µs per query with an empty delta (~47.6 ns per nominal path edge), +3% / +9% / +6% / +8% at 5,000 / 10,000 / 12,500 / 20,000 inserted (run B +15% / +5% / +7% / +12%); median MERGE latency 152 → 335 µs over the first 10,000 pending, 166 µs after the flush; one insert at #10,734 paid **24.2 ms** (29.4 ms in run B) — the flush.

**Verified against sources:** Revisiting Tables 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9 (every ratio recomputed); Sortledton §2–§7 and the code (merge threshold 0.45 vs the paper's 0.5); Teseo Equation (1) checked on the rendered page — the printed `high(W)` has `(τh + τ1)` where the code (`crawler.cpp:295`) and the leaf bound need `(τ1 − τh)`; PCSR Table I/II and the code; LiveGraph §2.1, §4, §6 and the code (Bloom filter from order 10 = 1,024 B, the paper says 256 B; entry 24 B vs the paper's 28 B); FalkorDB delta_wait.c / delta_mxm.c at `ccb449a9a`. Not verified: Aspen directly; the cause of the C engine's rising insert latency; the cause of the PMA's residual 2.6× scan gap; cache effects behind 1d's 2.14×.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 25 — neighbour sampling and aggregate-first, measured

Topic 25's README promised "GraphSAGE's real contribution" and the 90× associativity claim without a lane behind either. Added `sampling.rs` (seeded neighbour sampling, receptive-field counts, a preferential-attachment generator) and two lanes to `gnn_bench`. Counted, identical across two runs: for 256 seeds on 262,144-vertex graphs, SBM (avg deg 15.5, max 35) full 1/2/3 layers = 4,154 / 55,812 / 248,786 vertices (1.58% / 21.29% / 94.90%), sampled (25,10) = 36,895 vertices, 42,603 edges, sampled (15,10,5) = 125,781 / 187,073; preferential (avg 16.0, max 2,316) full = 4,165 / 114,729 / 262,089 vertices, 4,004 / 175,872 / 2,390,821 edges, sampled (25,10) = 29,451 / 34,977, (15,10,5) = 94,944 / 145,740. Sampling cuts 3-layer edges 4.9× (SBM) and 16.4× (hub graph); vertices only 2.0× / 2.8×. Aggregate-first vs transform-first, d_in 1433 → 16 on the 16,384-vertex SBM: counted 89.6× on the sparse side but 3.09× for the layer (the dense XW is paid either way); measured 80.2 vs 180.2 ms = 2.25× (second run 2.23×; a load-35 run 3.33×). README's "90× cheaper" now says which half.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 23 — posting-list compression, measured

README promised compression "that keeps random access" with no bytes behind it. Added `compress.rs` (varint deltas vs tantivy-shaped 128-block bit-packing with varint tail, round-trip tested on every list) and a lane in `fts_bench`. Exact bytes over 7,868,110 postings: raw 62.9 MB, varint 18.6 MB (2.363 B/posting), bit-packed 12.2 MB (1.544 B/posting, 5.2× vs raw, 1.53× vs varint). By df class: head (82 lists, 2,300,771 postings) 2.000 vs 0.786 B/posting; mid 2.060 vs 1.132; low 2.610 vs 1.635; tail (43,092 lists) 2.939 vs 2.939 (identical — no full block). Negative result: scalar decode of head lists is varint 1.66 ns/posting vs bit-packed 2.96 ns/posting (bit-packing is slower without SIMD; tantivy's crate is SIMD and is not measured). Not done: Elasticsearch scatter-gather and RRF hybrid fusion remain unmeasured (RRF needs the Cormack et al. SIGIR'09 PDF read before any figure is cited).

## 2026-10-26 — topic 48 — a two-tier emulation lane

The provided lane's ~1× at 5% hot never showed a tiering time difference. Added lane 1c: a fixed fast set (the 1,024 most-accessed 16 KiB pages of an earlier window, 16 MiB) and a calibrated dependent spin of 165.7 ns (SIDLE Fig. 1(c) 276.9 − 111.2 — a MODEL constant) on every access outside it; spin result folded into the next address through an opaque zero so it serialises; control = same loop, 0 iterations. Counted slow share, packed vs scattered: 0.1% 9.8% / 83.4%; 0.5% 9.9% / 96.6%; 1% 9.8% / 97.4%; 5% 71.7% / 98.2%. Timed (load 13–25): scattered/packed 4.58× / 4.62× / 3.24× / 1.35×; second run 4.42× / 5.74× / 4.07× / 1.26×. The tiered time lands within 16% of control + share × 165.7 (spin runs over its calibration in-loop; first attempt calibrated iterations only and was 18% over, so calibration now times whole calls). Cannot show bandwidth, queueing or prefetch effects of real CXL — stated in README. The earlier README row that rejected a latency-injected tier now says the lane uses it as a labelled model.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 20 — GxB_Container and the library boundary

Added `reading-gxb-container.md` (read at the pinned GraphBLAS commit `7701390417`, v10.5.2: `Doc/ChangeLog:147–164`, `Include/GraphBLAS.h:6896–7005`, `Source/container/GB_load_from_container.c`, `GB_unload_into_container.c`, `GB_vector_load.c`, `GB_vector_unload.c`, `Source/builder/GB_builder.c:44–66`) and a measured lane, `gb_container`, that links a real libgraphblas (10.5.0, from FalkorDB's native-deps cache) through a 100-line C shim and `build.rs` (cfg `have_graphblas`; the lane skips, exit 0, without one).

Lane A (RMAT edge-factor 8, seed 42; nnz 120,128 / 494,634 / 2,018,190 / 8,175,704; best of 5, load 24–26): Container load of the Rust CSR 0.17 / 0.12 / 0.17 / 0.12 µs, unload 0.12 at every size; `GrB_Matrix_build` sorted tuples 349 / 540 / 1,557 / 6,822 µs, shuffled 9,384 / 8,164 / 29,497 / 146,676 µs; `extractTuples` 118 / 251 / 1,320 / 4,039 µs; `memcpy` of the CSR 21 / 97 / 550 / 2,355 µs. Asserted in the lane: the pointers an unload returns are the Rust `Vec` pointers; GraphBLAS's own CSR (Ap, Ai both u32, sparse by-row) equals the seeded one entry for entry.

Lane B: Rust `spmv` 116 / 568 / 2,796 / 12,608 µs; `GrB_mxv` on the same matrix loaded via Container, 1 thread: JIT off 502 / 2,132 / 8,712 / 44,868 µs (3.1–4.3× slower; `GxB_BURBLE` shows the generic dot2 kernel), JIT on 150 / 452 / 2,647 / 11,905 µs (parity), 11 threads JIT on 65 / 191 / 759 / 2,864 µs (1.8–4.4× faster than the single-thread Rust kernel). sum(y) agrees to 1e-9 relative.

Negative/caveat: the JIT-off figure is the library without a compiled kernel (this install has no factory kernel on that path), not a statement about GraphBLAS in general; scale-14 single-thread JIT cells moved 63 → 150 µs between runs at different load.

- Topic 8 baseline fixed (2026-10-27): the provided `txn_bench` built each key's `String` inside the global mutex, which halved the lock and made it look flat (~600k txn/s on every mix). Keys and coin draws now happen before the lock. Re-measured, median of three: lock 1 393 945 / 806 155 / 1 092 582 txn/s (read-heavy / 50/50 10K / 50/50 64 hot); the reference MVCC 1 976 273 / 1 320 490 / 1 553 817 (1.42× / 1.64× / 1.42×, ~3 / ~230 / ~31 600 aborts). - Topic 8: wrote a reference MVCC in a scratch crate (RwLock over `HashMap<Key, Vec<Version>>`; passes all 8 tests; not committed). `txn_bench` with it, median of 3, 4 threads × 50 000 txns × 4 ops: read-heavy 600 842 → 2 119 487 txn/s (3.53×); 50/50 10K keys 526 678 → 1 353 137 (2.57×); 50/50 64 keys 576 817 → 1 640 987 (2.84×), ~31 800 aborts of 200 000 commits. Keyspace sweep (50/50): MVCC÷fair-lock 1.50× at 100 000 keys, 1.62× at 10 000, 1.82× at 1 000, 1.61× at 64, 1.34× at 16, 1.24× at 8, 1.06× at 4, 0.90× at 2, 0.70× at 1; aborts per commit 0.000, 0.001, 0.011, 0.154, 0.516, 0.833, 1.246, 1.496, 1.673. The provided baseline builds key strings inside the lock; the "fair" lock (~0.9 M txn/s) builds them outside. Single-mutex MVCC variant is 0.68× of the RwLock one at 64 keys. Serializable mode 0–25% slower, ~1.9× the aborts at 64 keys. README prediction (MVCC loses the hot row) did not reproduce; crossover at ~1 key per thread. Papers: SVCC (CIDR'27) is a position paper whose evidence is LoC (303/45/665 vs 426/265/1325, Table 1) and one TPC-C point (127K vs 119K txn/s, 8 threads, §3.3); cMVBT (arXiv preprint) is index-level, single-row transactions only, "preliminary" experiments reported as figures — both added to README §4 with those limits stated.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 50 — LLM-Era Query Processing: Semantic Operators, Text-to-Query, and Their Cost

**New topic 50, `LLM-Era Query Processing: Semantic Operators, Text-to-Query, and Their Cost`** — semantic operators (LLM-powered filter, join, map, top-k inside a plan), text-to-query and the agent as client, read as a cost-based optimization problem for an operator that costs dollars and seconds per row and is sometimes wrong. Package: README, six reading guides, `notes.md`, a dependency-free Rust crate (`semop_bench`) with lane 1 implemented and two lanes stubbed, `FINDINGS.md` row, `verify.sh` lane, `PLAN.md` §50 and a map node (`T10 --> T50 --> T38`), capstone M50, SUMMARY entries, `resources/papers.md` entries; topic count 46 → 47 and crate count 47 → 48 where they appear (sibling topics 46–49 are being built in parallel; the merge reconciles). `check-reading-depth.py --check --all` reads **247/247**.

**Lane 1 is a simulation and says so everywhere.** No LLM is called and nothing touches the network. Each predicate has a seeded ground truth, an oracle that is right 97% of the time (MODEL), and a proxy whose score is sigmoid(±μ + N(0,1)) (MODEL); every per-document fact is a pure hash of (seed, doc, predicate), so the oracle answers the same question identically (temperature 0) whatever order plans ask in. Constants: oracle $0.001 / $0.002 and 0.8 / 1.6 s per call (MODEL); proxy latency = oracle / 17.4 (LOTUS §5.4, one task); parallelism 20 (SemBench §5.1). Counts, selectivities and F1 reproduce exactly; dollars and seconds are counts × constants. Measured on Apple M3 Pro, but no figure depends on the machine.

```
plan                                    oracle   proxy   $ MODEL  s MODEL   rows  F1 truth  F1 gold
(a) every predicate, every row           20000       0     30.00     1200    364     0.835    1.000
    short-circuit, written order P2,P1   11235       0     21.23      850    364     0.835    1.000
    selectivity order P2,P1              11635       0     21.84      874    364     0.835    1.000
(b) rank order P1,P2 (+200-row sample)   13490       0     16.78      672    364     0.835    1.000
```

**Most calls, least money.** Ordering `WHERE P1 AND P2` by selectivity alone (the rare, expensive predicate first) makes the fewest oracle calls and costs $21.23; rank order (s − 1)/c makes 13,490 calls including 400 spent on a 200-row selectivity sample and costs **$16.78 (−21%)**. Sample estimates 0.315 / 0.130, ranks −685 / −435. **The oracle is not the truth**: truth has 297 rows, gold (oracle on every row) 364, overlap 276 — F1 0.835; closed form 0.822, mean over 30 other seeds 0.825. Sanity lines: short-circuit calls = 10,000 + 1,235 exactly; all orderings return identical rows.

**Lane 1c — negative result.** Cascade (proxy 88.7% / 82.1% right at a 0.5 cut), band [0.5 − h, 0.5 + h]: h = 0.30 → 7,702 oracle calls, F1 0.902 vs gold; h = 0.40 → 11,200, F1 0.978, $14.66 vs plan (b)'s $16.18 main pass — **9% saved**; h = 0.5 costs $0.65 *more* than no cascade. **Lane 1c′ constructs the case where it pays**, varying only μ: against truth, savings rise monotonically (−32% at μ = 1.0, −79% at 1.5, −96% once the proxy alone beats the oracle); against gold — LOTUS's frame — they peak at −20% (μ = 1.5) and vanish for μ ≥ 2.5 (+4%), because a proxy that agrees with the truth disagrees with the oracle's independent 3% errors. Flagged in the README as the finding most dependent on the simulation's independence assumption. **Lane 1d**: ten agents probing overlapping 2,000-row subsets — 26,150 oracle calls alone, **11,615** with a shared (doc, predicate) cache (= 8,878 distinct rows + 2,737 distinct P1-yes rows, exact), identical answers. **Lane 1e**: LIMIT 10 over P2 — 10,000 calls evaluated-then-limited vs 120 streamed in waves of 20, **83×** (10 / 0.1235 = 81, rounded to waves).

**Lane 2 (stub) — guaranteed cascade thresholds.** Provided row: thresholds that exactly fit a 300-row sample miss recall or precision 0.9 vs gold in **68 of 100** seeded tables (mean recall 0.903, precision 0.901, 2,204 band calls). Reference learners (written, run, recorded in notes.md, removed; stub tests green against them first): normal-approximation correction with δ/2 per threshold and a union bound over precision candidates 6% missed / 4,504 band calls; union bound over 10 candidates 7% / 4,281; none 10% / 4,076; Hoeffding 0% / 9,122 (valid, fails the < 6,000-call test — at ~93 sampled positives its radius 0.111 exceeds the whole 0.10 recall slack).

**Lane 3 (stub) — blocked semantic join.** Provided: nested loop over 300 × 200 = 60,000 pairs, $60, 2,400 s. Gold has 417 pairs = 390 the oracle got right + 27 it **invented** on non-matching pairs (real matches 398). No similarity blocking proposes the invented pairs, so recall against all of gold is capped (top-10: 0.916); the contract measures recall against gold ∩ truth. Reference per-left top-10: 3,000 oracle calls + 500 embeddings, $3.02, precision 1.000, recall 0.977 vs gold ∩ truth, passes on seeds 77–79; top-6 1,800 calls / 0.933 and top-8 2,400 / 0.954 fail the three-seed test; global thresholds 0.50 / 0.47 / 0.45 / 0.42 → 1,776 / 3,044 / 4,338 / 7,323 calls at recall-vs-gold 0.868 / 0.916 / 0.923 / 0.940.

**Paper claims, each checked against the text.** *Abacus* (Russo et al., PVLDB 19(5) 2026, arXiv 2505.14661): abstract's "10.8x cheaper and 3.4x faster" **checks out** as the means of Table 2's ratios (21.2 / 10.2 / 1.09; 5.22 / 4.04 / 1.05); "6.7%–39.4%" checks for BioDEX (+20.8%) and CUAD (+39.4%) but Table 2 gives MMQA +7.0%. **§4.3's prose does not match Table 2**: "20.3%, 18.7%, and 39.2%" and "12.6x cheaper and 2.7x faster" follow from no ordering of the table. §1's "up to 3.04x" with priors vs §4.4's 3.02×. Table 3 MinCost reductions recompute exactly (0.70/0.28 = 2.50, 0.51/0.12 = 4.25, 12.9/16.0 = 0.81 — the paper's own negative result); k = 6, j = 4, budget 50 × operators → 26 for exploration ✓. *LOTUS* (arXiv 2407.11418v3): FEVER 329.1 → 190.0 s = 1.73× at 91.0/91.2 = 99.8% ✓ (Table 2); 5,396.11/190.0 = 28× ✓; Table 5 gold 6,092,500 / 5,290 = 1,152× ("1,000×") ✓, Plan 2 RP@5 +37.6% / RP@10 +25.3% ✓; Table 7's quadratic 4,950 = C(100,2), 19,900 = C(200,2) ✓. Three self-contradictions recorded: abstract "up to 170%" vs intro "up to 100%" (different comparisons); Figure 9's caption and text swap which proxy is solid/dashed; the chosen join plan is 5,644 calls / 2,340 s in Table 3 and 5,290 / 2,116 s in Table 5. *SemBench* (Lao et al., PVLDB 19(8) 2026): LIMIT >100× (§5.3), 40,000 calls = 200² and 160,000 = 400² (§5.3–5.4), Table 5 join row (0.703/$3.29, 0.614/$2.74, 0.487/$0.14, 0.601/$1.02) ✓; **§5.3 says Movie ran at "a small scale of 500", Table 3 says 2,000**; the "48.1s" BigQuery latency is the Filter+Join row, not the all-operator 31.7 s. *Text-to-SQL Benchmarks are Broken* (Jin et al., CIDR'26): 80/121 = 66.1% ✓; 52.8% of 498 = 263, consistent with Table 1 (77/263 = 29.3%, 152/263 = 57.8%) ✓; CHESS 62 → 81 = +30.6% ✓, OpenSearch-SQL 71 → 69 = −2.8% ✓. **Not consistent**: prior-work rate given as both 32% and 36.1% for the same reference [2] (102 additional errors → 161/498 = 32.3%); **Table 4's flips net +22 (CHESS) and −4 (OpenSearch-SQL) against EX changes of +19 and −2** on a 100-problem sample. *Agent-first* (Liu et al., CIDR'26): Table 1 reductions recompute to −14.2 / −27.8 / −36.7 / −16.7 / −18.1% (paper truncates three); activity rows sum to 12.54 / 9.28, not the "all SQL queries" 12.67 / 10.38; the summary box's ">20%" holds for two of four phases, overall is 18.1%; 14–70% and "<10–20%" are read from figures only; Neon 20× branches / 50× rollbacks is an observation (§6.2). *LLM as cardinality estimator* (Liu, Wang, Wu et al., arXiv 2603.28080; the PDF names no venue, so "VLDB'26" was not confirmed): selective invocation on ~25–35% of sub-queries cuts inference ~66% and wins end to end on every workload (§10.1.3); full invocation loses on JOB-light and ErgastF1. The arXiv extended version of Jin et al. (2601.08778) was not read; every Jin number is from the CIDR PDF.

**Anchors.** Two new clones, pinned with the sticky `pin-table.py` (run with `DLP_CLONES` pointing at a view of `~/repos` that excludes sibling agents' fresh `delta`, `f3` and `ducklake` clones, so this commit adds only its own rows): `lotus-data/lotus` at `136ae4f` (1.2.4) and `mitdbg/palimpzest` at `807ed30` (1.5.3; Abacus is its optimizer, `README.md:55`, `optimization.mdx:22`). Every anchor checked with `pinned-source.py`; all 108 quoted snippet lines diffed against the pinned files, 0 mismatches. Findings in LOTUS at the pin: `CascadeArgs` defaults are γ = 0.8, δ = 0.2, 10% sample, not the paper's evaluation settings (`types.py:164-167`); importance sampling draws only from the **first 200 entries** it is handed, with replacement (`cascade_utils.py:20-26`; a numpy replication of lines 16–26 for N = 10,000 drew 1,000 indices, max 199, 194 distinct), and `sem_filter` uses them as row positions (`sem_filter.py:510`) with caching off by default (`settings.py:24`); the recall correction divides per-side means (`cascade_utils.py:120`) — worked to give 0.953 or 0.971 for the same 84/93 recall depending on the split; precision candidates share the full δ (`:133`), so the code's failure budget sums to 2δ; the join optimizer compares plans on band size only (`sem_join.py:533`) though it pays both plans' samples and the map pass. In Palimpzest: cost = per-record cost × input cardinality with selectivity propagated (`cost_model.py:252, 260`) — the predicate-ordering effect of lane 1a, not Equation 1's flat sum; join quality = op quality × the *mean* of its inputs (`models.py:965`), not the product; the bandit adds selectivity as a fourth metric (`mab_execution_strategy.py:473, 544`).

**Gates.** `./verify.sh 50` PASS; `check-reading-depth.py --check --all` 247/247; `mdbook build` + `check-book-links.py` 6,021 internal links, 0 dead; `check-mermaid.js` 3 blocks (topic 50 + PLAN), 0 errors; `RUSTFLAGS="-D warnings" cargo build --release` clean; `cargo test --release`: 12 provided tests pass, 5 stub tests fail as the specification.
## 2026-10-26 — topic 49 — Open File & Table Formats: Parquet's Successors, Iceberg/Delta/DuckLake, and Transactions on Object Storage

Topic 12 (encodings) and topic 28 (object stores) both ended on "Newer work"
items — F3 and LakeVilla — pointing at a layer the curriculum had not built:
the table format. Topic 49 builds it. Branch `topic/49-table-formats` from
`audit/2026-10-refresh`.

**Lane 1, provided** (`table_bench`; Apple M3 Pro; counts seeded and exact,
timings local at load average 7.7–14.5). 1,000 one-file commits, 100 per day
partition, into an Iceberg-shaped tree and a Delta-shaped log on a metered
in-memory object store (PUT, put-if-absent, CAS, GET, LIST paged at 1,000
keys, dependent rounds).

- Planning the latest snapshot after 10 / 100 / 1,000 commits: Iceberg fast
  append 13 / 103 / 1,003 GETs (1,373,885 B at 1,000); Iceberg merge append
  (Java's default, min-count-to-merge 100) 13 / 4 / 13; Delta without
  checkpoints 11 / 101 / 1,001; Delta checkpointed every 10: 11 / 11 / 11
  (399,735 B). Rounds 4 (Iceberg) vs 3 (Delta) at every size.
- One-day query at 1,000: fast append 103 GETs (900 manifests pruned by
  partition summaries), merge append 4, Delta 11 — the same 399,735 B as a
  full scan. Time travel to v500: Iceberg 503, Delta 500 / 10.
- Metadata written by the 1,000 commits: Iceberg fast append 398,749,323 B
  (195,826,990 table metadata + 202,285,853 manifest lists + 587,480 manifests
  + 49,000 pointer), merge append 219,263,669, Delta 522,593 without
  checkpoints and 20,307,084 with. 500,500 entries per rewritten kind →
  391.3 B per snapshot entry, 404.2 B per manifest-list entry; Delta's 99
  checkpoints write 49,599 adds at 398.9 B.
- After maintenance: Iceberg rewrite manifests 4 GETs (514,082 B after
  expiring to 1 snapshot — and time travel to v500 is gone); Delta fresh
  checkpoint 2 GETs + 1 LIST. Local CPU to plan: Iceberg 6,114 µs, Delta every
  10 1,949 µs.
- Lane 1c, Parquet footer (parquet 59.1.0 = the arrow-rs pin's version): 100
  / 1,000 / 10,000 / 50,000 float columns → 10,843 / 113,461 / 1,157,462 /
  6,027,910 B (108.4 → 120.6 B/column); full decode 36 / 353 / 3,828 / 19,484
  µs; skipping statistics for all but 10 columns 41 / 355 / 3,868 / 20,037 —
  **not faster**; cached schema 23 / 231 / 2,436 / 12,768.

**Lane 2 (stub):** concurrent writers on put-if-absent. Reference, 25 ops per
writer: conflicts per commit 0.00 / 0.52 / 1.07 / 2.04 / 3.29 at 1 / 2 / 4 / 8
/ 16 appenders with zero aborts; 2 compactors 10 committed, 4 aborted, 36
skipped; 4/2/2 mixed 160 committed, 272 conflicts, 26 aborted. Six stub tests,
run green against the reference before stubbing; the audit (no double
remove, contiguous versions) is provided.

**Lane 3 (stub):** checkpoint policy, cost = bytes + 250,000 per request (5 ms
× 50 MB/s, Delta VLDB'20 §2.3 low ends), 600 commits, random seeded reads.
Every-10 219.3 / 434.7 / 2,577.8 M at 0.02 / 0.2 / 2 reads per commit; best
fixed 215.7 (k=20) / 393.4 (k=5) / 1,530.0 (k=2); **ski rental (provided) is
the negative result**, 305.3 at 0.02 (1.42× best) because rent arrives in
lumps; EOQ reference 221.5 / 388.0 / 1,530.0. A first version with evenly
spaced reads let fixed intervals alias with the reads (k=50 "won"); reads were
made random.

**Six guides.** Specs (Iceberg @ 47677ab, v1–v3 adopted, v4 in development;
Delta PROTOCOL.md @ a7d0822; DuckLake 1.1 @ ducklake-web 42237d5): the spec's
"O(1) remote calls" goal fails for fast append until manifests are merged;
DuckLake manifesto claims (sub-ms inlined writes, thousands of tx/s, millions
of snapshots) labelled unmeasured. iceberg-rust @ 26356f5: fast append only;
every append's default duplicate check reads every live manifest (499,500
GETs over lane 1 by arithmetic, not charged by the lane); a retry rewrites
manifest and manifest list; defaults 4 retries / 100 ms / ×2. delta-rs @
26ea54f: default checkpoint interval **100**, not the paper's 10; the
DynamoDB S3 lock is gone in favour of conditional put; the conflict checker
skips `dataChange: false` removes, so two concurrent compactions of a file can
both commit (code reading, not run — a notes.md open question). LakeVilla:
Table 4 verified (LV 2.81 / 21.92%, LV[Write] 2.79 / 29.42% against the
prose's "ca. 20%"). F3: Fig. 15 annotates 10.0–29.2% (max on log) against the
prose's "9.6% … up to 25% on classic"; Fig. 9 shows ≈3–6× against "an order of
magnitude"; Fig. 13 shows 52.0% for zstd strings beyond the intro's 10–30%.
Parquet footer @ arrow-rs fed7862 (byte-identical to crates.io 59.1.0): skip
still walks every field; the policy costs a `HashSet` lookup per column.

New clones pinned with the sticky pin table: `iceberg`, `iceberg-rust`,
`delta`, `delta-rs`, `f3`, `ducklake`, `ducklake-web`.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 48 — Memory Tiering: CXL, Disaggregated Memory & Placement

No topic priced the memory tier behind DRAM, and an in-memory graph engine hits
that wall first. This machine has no CXL and no NUMA, so the topic measures the
mechanism every tiering policy depends on at the tier boundary it does have —
cache in front of DRAM — and quotes the papers' CXL numbers with sections. A
latency-injected "far tier" was rejected: it would measure the spin loop.

**Lane 1, provided (Apple M3 Pro, load average 7.21 — sibling benchmarks were
running all session; no run was possible below 7).** Lane 1a, dependent-load
ladder (Sattolo permutation, median of 5): 1.18 ns at 16 KiB, 4.34 at 256 KiB,
8.85 at 4 MiB, 14.95 at 8 MiB, 44.02 at 16 MiB, 90.03 at 32 MiB, 116.29 at
512 MiB. Lane 1b: 1 GiB of 64 B objects, 90% of accesses to a hot set, the same
seeded ids over a packed and a scattered layout (checksums agree on every row),
timed in alternation; loop floor 7.96 ns, all-cold 129.28 ns. ns/access
packed → scattered: 0.1% hot 26.98 → 37.03 (1.37×); 0.5% 37.19 → 86.12
(2.35×); 1% 60.79 → 110.73 (1.76×); 5% 114.96 → 126.76 (1.07×). Exact counts,
1% hot set: 90% of accesses on 655 16 KiB pages packed vs 55,806 scattered
(hot-page utilization 100.0% vs 3.4%); at 4 KiB, 2,619 vs 149,206 pages (10.2
vs 582.8 MiB); scattered hot set touches 65,536 of 65,536 16 KiB pages. Four
runs' scattered/packed spread: 0.1% 1.37–1.87×, 0.5% 1.87–2.82×, 1% 1.37–2.19×,
5% 0.93–1.07× (plus one 2.01× at load 10.95 that the sanity check flagged —
223.6 ns against a 111.3 ns DRAM rung). The 8 and 16 MiB rungs moved from
9.76/17.28 to 58.90/105.39 ns between runs: the shared L2's effective size is
whatever other processes leave.

**Lane 2 (stub, counted):** learn a layout from one window. Reference
(frequency packing): 655 pages for 90% (oracle 655, scattered 55,800); after
half the hot set drifts, scattered 47,467, oracle 47,561, learned 46,506.
**Lane 3 (stub, MODEL, SIDLE Fig. 1(c) prices 111.2 / 276.9 ns, 189 ns per
4 KiB page moved):** first touch 93.1% / 30.5% fast hits (packed / scattered),
122.6 / 226.4 ns; promote-on-touch 134.1 / 467.7 ns; oracle static 120.4 /
213.8 ns; reference TPP 96.9% / 34.4% hits, 124.0 / 293.0 ns — the model's
negative result: TPP's migrations cost more than its hits save at these
prices, and hysteresis cuts promotions 3.8× (681,004 vs 2,569,245). Eight stub
tests were green against the references before stubbing; 10 provided tests
pass.

**Papers, verified.** Hash Joins Meet CXL (CIDR'26): 30.01 / 20.95 / 47.50
GB/s, 3 : 2 (§4.1); Eq. 1 checked; Table 1 PHJ x* reproduces (0.5532 vs
0.5528) but g does not quite (1.1665 vs 1.1703); **NPHJ does not reproduce**
(x* 0.4789 vs 0.5104 from its own k, m); measured gains (22.20%, 3.59% runtime
= 1.285×, 1.037×) exceed the model's stated upper bound. TPP (ASPLOS'23):
Figure 2 80–140 / 170–250 ns; 55–80% idle (§2); Table 1 checked — the
abstract's "18%" is default Linux's largest loss (Cache2 1:4 at 82.0%), "5–17%"
are percentage points, and performance was measured on dual-socket servers
emulating CXL (§6). SIDLE (VLDB'26): Figure 1(c) gives 2.49× latency and 65.6%
bandwidth where the text says "~2×" and "~60%"; 82/85% hottest-node share
(Figure 5); 71% / 81% (abstract); genuine CXL 1.1. OBASE (OSDI'26): Bravo 3.2% of
bytes / 91.8% of pages; 16.3 GiB @ 21% → 6.33 GiB @ 57% (3.42 vs 3.61 GiB used
— consistent); TPP 1.65 → 1.85× and 1.25 → 1.45×; slow tier is Optane PMem, not
CXL; graphs out of scope (§3.2). Quoted only: Weisgut et al. (VLDB'25 §4.4,
520 ns FPGA CXL random reads), SHARD (VLDB'26 §1, 2–3 µs RDMA), MAC (OSDI'26:
2.4×, 24%/40% metadata, 98%). Declarative Memory Services (CIDR'26) skimmed: a
vision paper with no numbers to check.

**Code read:** `fukien/hashjoins-meet-cxl` at `0aeea0d` (new clone, pinned with
the sticky pin-table). Placement is `mmap` + `mbind` (`MPOL_BIND`,
`MPOL_WEIGHTED_INTERLEAVE`) with system-wide sysfs weights; the scripts' ratio
is 10 : 7, not 3 : 2; **the paper's partial-move strategy is not in the
release** (`hc_join` called by the scripts, never built; `phj.h:362` commented
out; `common.c:5` fraction globals unused); every strategy times a full copy,
in-CXL included; `memcpy_tps.c` copies 8 GiB under a "4 GB" comment and prints
GiB/s as GB/s; the `algos[]` lookup has no sentinel.

Five guides (hash-join paper, its code, TPP, SIDLE, OBASE). Plumbing: PLAN §48 +
map node, FINDINGS row, verify.sh lane, PROGRESS + M48, SUMMARY, papers.md,
topic count 46 → 47, crate count 47 → 48. Gates: `./verify.sh 48` PASS;
`check-reading-depth.py --check --all` 246/246; mermaid 7 blocks 0 errors;
book links 0 dead (5,998 checked); `-D warnings` build clean.
## 2026-10-26 — topic 47 — Semi-join Reduction in Practice: Yannakakis, Predicate Transfer & Bitmap Filters

Built from topic 10's "Newer work" items (RPT+, SQL Server bitmaps, the IN
paper) and topic 44's Yannakakis thread, taken deep. Four papers downloaded
and read in full: Predicate Transfer (CIDR'24), RPT+ (VLDB'26), SQL Server
bitmap filters (CIDR'26), IN predicates (CIDR'26). Yannakakis VLDB'81 could
not be obtained (VLDB 1981 archive: HTTP 403; no other copy found); its
guarantees are quoted via PT §2.2, RPT+ §2.1 and CIDR'26 §2.1 and checked by
the crate's tests, and no number is attributed to it.

**Lane 1 (provided).** Seeded social graph (seed 47): knows 200,000, likes
100,000, has_tag 100,000, rare_tag 10 of 1,000 tags; the three-hop chain
`knows ⋈ likes ⋈ has_tag ⋈ rare_tag`, OUT = 20,210, checksum
a77191f1c5106d95 in all four plans. Good order: 1,000 / 2,036 / 20,210 =
23,246 intermediates, 400,000 build rows. Bad order: 1,000,000 / 2,000,000 /
20,210 = 3,020,210 (149.44 × OUT), 200,010 build rows. After a full
reduction rooted at knows: good 868 / 2,036 / 20,210 = 23,114; bad 20,084 /
20,210 / 20,210 = 60,504 (2.99 × OUT). Bad ÷ good 129.9× → 2.62×. Reduction:
forward 3,035 inserts + 400,000 probes, backward 22,233 + 3,035; 428,303
hash ops = 1.07 × N (N = 400,010). Sizes base / after forward / after both:
knows 200,000 / 19,340 / 19,340; likes 100,000 / 2,025 / 2,025; has_tag
100,000 / 1,000 / 868; rare_tag 10. Hand predictions: knows′ ≈ 19,100;
has_tag after backward ≈ 865 (e⁻²). Timings (best of 3, load 4.39 at start,
other agents benchmarking): good 6.94 ms, bad 37.94, reduce 1.50, good after
0.54, bad after 0.52; bad plan 80 M tuples/s. Earlier runs at load 4.52 and
8.16: bad 35.90 and 44.61 ms. Negative result: on the good order the
reduction costs 428,303 hash ops to save 132 intermediates; and the good
order is slower in time than reduce + bad order because it builds 400,000
rows.

**Lane 1b (provided).** 10⁶ absent keys, equal bits: 1,000 keys / 16,384
bits: classic 0.00045 (formula 0.00038), register-blocked DuckDB layout
0.00548; 10,000 / 131,072: 0.00181 (0.00184) vs 0.00901; 100,000 /
2,097,152: 0.00006 (0.00004) vs 0.00249. The classic filter at 20.97
bits/key matches RPT+'s CSBF 6.1 × 10⁻⁵ at 20 (§5.4).

**Lane 2 (stub; reference written, run, removed).** Bloom predicate
transfer, survivors / not exact / filter FP / inherited / bad-order
intermediates: 2 bits/key 264,558 / 242,315 / 42,144 / 200,171 / 839,862;
4: 198,190 / 175,947 / 24,189 / 151,758 / 436,532; 8: 58,087 / 35,844 /
6,004 / 29,840 / 106,100; 16: 22,851 / 608 / 159 / 449 / 60,960 (exact
22,243 rows, 60,504 intermediates). Per-key FPR at 8 bits/key 0.0221–0.0227
vs formula 0.0214–0.0215. Inherited rows are 5× the filters' own false
positives at 8 bits/key — the term PT §3.5's model omits (it predicts 3,884
own FPs for knows; measured 3,340, plus 28,648 inherited). First draft of the
FPR test measured per row and failed on a 10-key filter; FPR is per key.

**Lane 3 (stub; reference written, run, removed).** Exact bitmaps, left-deep
plans: bad + into lower builds 3,035 build rows, 400,000 bitmap probes,
60,504 intermediates (= full reduction, one pass — CIDR'26 §3.3 reproduced);
bad + probe spine only 3,020,210 (no help); good + into lower builds 386,439
build rows, 23,114; good + probe spine only 400,000, 23,246.

**Paper claims checked.** RPT+: 1.47/1.28/1.17/1.01× (Figure 11) hold; RPT
28.1% and RPT+ 2.1% regressions (Tables 1–2) hold; τ_stop is 0.9 in §5.4/§7.2
but 0.95 in §5.3/Figure 9; "up to 500×" is figure prose (Table 2's >100×
bucket averages 226.29×). SQL Server: 7 of 22 > 2× (max 3.47×), 12
instance-optimal (prose only — check marks lost in extraction), 5 optimizer
slowdowns, Q12 2.96 M vs 2.92 M; "deployed since 2012" vs "shipped in SQL
Server 2014" inconsistent; "~900 rows" per rowgroup not used. PT: §4.2
averages hold per scale factor; **the introduction's "4.8× (up to 47×)" over
Yannakakis does not hold as stated** — 4.8× is SF10 while the paired 3.3× is
SF1, and the only 47× is Q2 vs NoPredTrans. IN paper: survey and Figure 10's
9 ms / 15 ms hold.

**Code read.** `duckdb` — no local clone existed; made a blobless clone at
the pinned `6c0c1a68` (pin unchanged). At the pin DuckDB builds Bloom
filters (register-blocked, ≥ 12 bits/key), exact range bitmaps and IN-lists
for join keys, unlike the v1.3.0 RPT+ measured against; filters reach only
the probe spine (`join_filter_pushdown_optimizer.cpp:156-160`, `:216-219`);
the selectivity-optional pause (`table_filter_selectivity_optional_function.cpp:25-46`)
as written never resumes once paused (hand trace + transcription; not run in
DuckDB). `datafusion` at `1e77af8`: dynamic filters routed by column
ownership into build sides too (`exec.rs:1675-1693`); above 150 keys / 128 KiB
the filter is the build's hash table (`exec.rs:2106-2129`). 32 anchors
checked with `pinned-source.py check`.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 46 — Graph Queries on Relational Engines: SQL/PGQ, Factorized Execution & Path-Query Planning

The deep version of what topics 10, 11 and 13 carried as one-paragraph
"Newer work" notes: graph patterns inside relational engines, the blow-up that
flat joins pay for them, and the 2026 work that removes it.

**Lane 1, provided (Apple M3 Pro).** Seeded integer-only power-law generator
(SplitMix64; both endpoints copied from earlier endpoints 70% of the time;
sorted + deduplicated, so counts are bit-identical everywhere). 2-hop and 3-hop
path patterns as left-deep pipelined hash joins (tuples produced), CSR
expansion, and factorized size from degrees:

- |V| = 10,000 / 77,164 edges: 2-hop 6,665,086 tuples vs 161,624 f-rep values
  (124×); 3-hop 241,301,569 tuples (958,541,190 values) vs 6,738,545 (142×).
- |V| = 20,000 / 155,727: 17,759,290 vs 326,298 (163×); 739,497,555
  (2,940,230,930 values) vs 17,875,125 (164×).
- |V| = 40,000 / 313,512: **47,900,847 vs 656,622 (219×); 2,272,724,241
  tuples (9,042,996,117 values) vs 47,997,149 (188×)** — the headline.
- Uniform control (0% preferential), |V| = 40,000 / 319,962 edges, max out 20:
  2,560,772 vs 679,805 (11×); 23,058,939 vs 3,240,016 (28×). Skew is the premise.
- FFX's Fig. 1a reproduced: 13 tuples, 39 values, 22 over T1, 15 over T2.
- Timings at load {4.28 4.07 4.32}, best of 3: 3-hop at 40K hash join 624.2 ms,
  CSR 464.3 ms (1.30–1.35× in every row, same tuple count), degree-product count
  0.45 ms; 0.21–0.35 ns per produced tuple (a lower bound: the plan writes
  nothing). Re-run at load {12.47 8.19 8.18}: up to 2.3× slower, counts identical.

**Lane 2 (stub): factorized join operator** on FFX-style packed offset vectors
— provided 2-hop build (656,622 values, 4.2 ms); stubbed bottom-insert
`extend`, `count`, `flatten`, `retain_d` with cascade. Reference (written, run,
removed): 47,997,149 values = closed form in 20.7 ms; count 2,224,823,394 in
0.044 ms; `retain_d(d % 8 == 0)` → 6,632,319 values, 286,772,642 tuples in
26.0 ms. Build + count ≈ 25 ms vs 624.2 ms flat.

**Lane 3: RPQ `a·b*`** from 257 sources (78,165 a-edges, 235,347 b-edges).
Provided semi-naive `WITH RECURSIVE … UNION`: 6,536,711 pairs, 10 rounds,
40,832,336 tuples derived, 675.9 ms. Walks vs pairs: k=4 6,679,113,406 vs
5,845,226 (1,142.7×); k=8 7.4×10⁹×. Stub: product-automaton BFS. Reference:
same pairs, 6,536,968 states, **40,832,336 edges scanned — exactly the
semi-naive derived count** (set semantics already makes the recursion a BFS;
the difference is cost per step, 134.2 ms, 5.0×).

**Five guides; claims verified and the ones that did not hold.**
- Lehner & Neumann (VLDB'26): §2.2's 62.5% (6/16), §4.5's count formula on
  Fig. 3 (10 tuples), §5.1's ID example, §6.1 break-evens (10/12/36 duplicates,
  BI 1.9× TI), §6.2 (−15% all acyclic, +6.9% >10 ms, +52% >100 ms, cyclic −32%,
  vs Umbra −58%), §6.4 (18× parallel, BI in 49% of queries). **Not
  reconstructable:** the abstract's "over 25×" (only on Figure 15's log axis)
  and "over 19% of CE queries" (19% of 2,557 = 486 matches no §6.2 count).
- FFX (SIGMOD'26): §2.2.1's 13/39/22/15 reproduced in code; Fig. 4 offsets;
  §7.2 1,883 of 1,951 plans (printed 96.53%/3.47%, computed 96.51%/3.49%).
  **Table 4 prose mismatches:** caption says seconds, values are ms; "up to
  108.4×" fewer calls vs 114.13× in the table; "1.35–3.59×" cycles mixes rows
  and omits 8.14×. LSQB comparisons use hand-picked orders (§7.3.3).
- CRPQ planning (VLDB'26): Table 5's "average speedup" 2.0833 / 1.6833 is the
  ratio of average times (5.5544/2.6662, 5.3256/3.1638); timeouts 186 → 60 on
  WDBench (−67.7%), unchanged 10 → 10 on LDBC SNB. Eqs. 37, 38, 42 worked.
- DuckPGQ (CIDR'23 + `cwida/duckpgq-extension` @ `29ca668`, new clone, pinned):
  `MATCH` → key equalities (`match.cpp:486–495`); CSR by atomic counting sort;
  512-lane bitset BFS; a bounded quantifier compiles to
  `iterativelength(...) BETWEEN l AND u` — a filter on *shortest* distance,
  one row per pair (read from code, not run); the October 2026
  `FactorizedJoinOptimizer` (degree-product `count(*)`, two-sided n:m test).
  TurboLynx Table 4: DuckPGQ 15,905 ms vs DuckDB 3,467 ms on LDBC SF100 (same
  84-query denominator, 4.6×). **TurboLynx Table 4's Umbra row does not
  check:** printed 7.74× / 0.58× vs ms quotients 27.6 / 9.26 (DBpedia agrees);
  DuckPGQ SF100 printed 183.9 vs 189.3; Kùzu DBpedia 18.88 vs 17.88. Topic 13's
  README quotes the Umbra ratios — left as is, flagged here.
- PostgreSQL SQL/PGQ at `2f094e7ac69` (reverted `b1f106c80cb` /
  `2b9e1aff4d3`; in no release), read with `pinned-source.py --ref`: one join
  query per element-table combination (product over factors — the cause of
  follow-ups `2ff289d0393` stack depth and `a82e33a83d7` interruptibility),
  `UNION ALL` (`:728`), quantifiers refused (`parse_graphtable.c:233–236`).

## 2026-10-26 — repo-wide review — a pin that moved itself, three benches that measured the wrong thing, and what upstream did since July

A review of the whole repo against today's upstreams and literature, three
months after most pins were taken. Topic 45 (below) is the new topic it
seeded; everything else is here.

**The C FalkorDB anchors had stopped resolving.** Topic 44's regeneration of
the pin table ran while `~/repos/FalkorDB` was on the Rust engine and moved the
FalkorDB pin from `ccb449a9a` (C) to `aa75821ab`, a tree with no `src/graph/`;
`pinned-source.py check FalkorDB src/graph/delta_matrix/delta_matrix.h:108`
failed while four guides still named `ccb449a9a`. Restored, and fixed at the
cause: `tools/pin-table.py` now keeps every existing pin, re-pins only on
`--bump NAME`, keeps rows for clones deleted from this machine (seven had been,
and a regeneration would have dropped them), and reports drifted clones
without rewriting them.

**The published book had 85 dead links**, every one a `.../README.md` link that
mdbook renders as a `README.html` it never writes — 43 of them in FINDINGS.md.
A preprocessor (`tools/mdbook-readme-links.py`) rewrites them in the book's copy
only. Two CI steps were added for what CI could not see: every internal link
and anchor in the rendered HTML (`tools/check-book-links.py`: 5,857 links, 0
dead) and every diagram through mermaid's own parser (`tools/check-mermaid.js`:
91 blocks, 0 errors; a deliberately broken control fails).

**Three benchmarks were measuring something other than what they claimed.**
- Topic 27: `ChurnGen` drew deletions by seeded index into a `HashSet`'s
  iteration order, which std randomizes per process — 1364 vs 1365 triangles
  and 21,062,928 vs 21,063,216 wedges on consecutive runs. Now sorted: 1365 /
  21,063,140 on every run.
- Topic 42: the same pattern in the collaboration generator's triadic closure
  and in `personalization()`'s sample. Now 0.353 / 0.157 / 0.922 and 0.403 /
  0.815 / 0.457 on every run, random predictor 0.313%; lane 3's references
  re-derived (PA 1.0×, common neighbours 21.7×, Jaccard 27.9×, Adamic/Adar
  24.0×). And the July audit's "correction" of FINDINGS row 42 to 34.0% / 92.3%
  was itself the error: the crate as first committed gives 0.353 / 0.922 today,
  and 0.340 / 0.923 never came from committed code. The original 35.3% / 92.2%
  is restored.
- Topic 32: lane 1's write p99 was 20.3 s, then 37.5 s, then 3.2 s and 22.2 s
  on consecutive runs of a "2 s" window — `std::sync::Mutex` on macOS is
  `os_unfair_lock`, the scanner re-took the lock before the woken writer ran,
  and the lane measured starvation. With `parking_lot::FairMutex` it measures
  the HTAP problem itself: p99 209 ns → 2.25 ms (= 2 s / 889 scans, one scan),
  14,052,932 → 21,543 writes, reproducing within 1% over three runs.
A full double run of `./verify.sh`, diffed, found no other count that differs
between runs.

**Upstream since the pins**, as ~45 "Upstream since the pin" callouts with
anchors left at the pin, each checked against code and release tags: Redis
8.8–8.10 rax rework and the unstable B+-tree sorted sets; Valkey 9.2-rc1's
fbtree ordered index, SPSC/SPMC I/O-thread jobs and Forkless Save (full sync
still forks — read from `startBgsaveForReplication`); qdrant v1.19–1.20
(the `m0 != 0` gate went in 1.20.0, not 1.19.2); DuckDB 2.0.0 (44 optimizer
calls, +5; `skip_scan` gone); DataFusion range repartitioning; Polars 2.x;
RocksDB 11.13's PerfContext change (attributed, not measured); Turso
`wal_autocheckpoint`; ClickHouse adaptive codec selection, which contradicts a
guide's "nothing analyzes". FalkorDB 6.0.0 (2026-09-29) is the Rust engine on
`main`; the C engine continues on `master`, and `ccb449a9a` is pre-split C
history. Kùzu is archived at exactly its pin. PostgreSQL 19 does not ship
SQL/PGQ: committed as 2f094e7ac69, reverted as b1f106c80cb / 2b9e1aff4d3.
Seven claims turned out to be wrong at the pin itself and were corrected
(qdrant's "≈2M points scored", "three quantization families", DataFusion's
two partitioning schemes, DuckDB's "exactly two" selectivity sites, an
unsourced "~1 ns" PerfContext bump, a rax snippet that elided the line its
prose pointed at, an unqualified Valkey ignition rule).

**Newer work.** 39 papers from 2025–2026, each downloaded and read, added to
22 topic READMEs with section/table citations and to resources/papers.md.
The papers corrected their own leads more than once: TurboLynx's 183.9× is over
DuckPGQ only and it is 0.58× Umbra on TPC-H; RPT+ is built on DuckDB 1.3.0, not
shipped in it; S-RocksDB's abstract pairs two settings (like-for-like 64.3M →
10,206 stalled writes); TVA's "9.9×" is nowhere in its evaluation. Five could
not be read and were left out (cache_ext, Fawkes, ValScope, Aquila, CGIF).

**Notes.** Topics 26–43 now open with a dated baseline from this day's run and
an empty prediction worksheet; the author's predictions moved into collapsed
blocks.

**Capstone baselines.** Re-measured against clean, tagged builds of both
shipping engines — C FalkorDB v4.22.2 (7b61f511d) and Rust FalkorDB v6.0.3
(c937aa4fa) — 100 rounds, median and p99, plus memory. Against the C bar, Rust
is 1.18–1.60× slower on bulk creates, level on matches (0.94–0.99×), 0.34–0.62×
on deletes, and 3–22% lower on peak RSS (1M edges: 693 vs 879 MiB). Load
average 2.9–3.4 during the runs, recorded.

## 2026-10-26 — topic 45 — Async I/O & Modern SSDs: the interface is not the win

**New topic 45, `Async I/O & Modern SSDs: io_uring, Out-of-Place Writes, and When They Help`** — the topic that prices what topics 5, 6, 7 and 19 only named. Package: README, five reading guides, `notes.md`, a Rust crate with lane 1 implemented and two lanes stubbed, `FINDINGS.md` row, `verify.sh` lane, `PLAN.md` §45 and a map node (`T6 --> T45`), capstone M45, SUMMARY entries, `resources/papers.md` entries. `check-reading-depth.py --check --all` reads **241/241**.

**The measured headline is queue depth, not an interface.** A 1 GiB seeded file written and read around the page cache (`F_NOCACHE` + `F_RDAHEAD 0` on macOS, `O_DIRECT` on Linux), 20,000 fresh seeded random 4 KiB reads per row, queue depth bought with blocking `pread` threads, Apple M3 Pro internal SSD (APPLE SSD AP0512Z):

```
    QD      IOPS    MiB/s   p50 µs   p99 µs  mean µs   predicted  achieved
     1     13810       54     68.8     80.6     72.2       13843      100%
     2     27155      106     69.5    110.6     73.5       27686       98%
     4     52450      205     72.0    115.9     76.1       55373       95%
     8     96907      379     74.0    155.5     82.4      110746       88%
    16    155741      608     91.5    199.4    102.4      221491       70%
    32    244300      954    112.5    316.5    130.3      442983       55%
    64    248363      970    203.0    583.9    254.3      885965       28%
```

**18× through the same system call.** The predicted column is Little's law with QD-1 latency held fixed; fed each row's own mean latency the law closes to 0.2% at QD 8 (8 / 82.4 µs = 97,087 vs 96,907) and 1.3% at QD 64 (251,671 vs 248,363) — past QD 16 the device queues and extra in-flight reads buy latency. Three sanity lines are part of the output: QD-1 p50 68.8 µs above the 8 µs cold-read floor; best random row 970 MiB/s = 16% of the measured sequential ceiling of 5,967 MiB/s (1 MiB reads, 8 threads); `pread`, 8 threads and `lio_listio` return the same checksum `9786a6be5036cf04` over 2,000 shared offsets. Run-to-run on this machine: QD-1 13,330–13,810, QD-64 237,589–248,363, ceiling 5,967–6,180 MiB/s.

**Lane 1b — a batch barrier halves throughput.** POSIX AIO `lio_listio(LIO_WAIT)`: batch 1 / 4 / 16 → 13,385 / 39,273 / 79,200 IOPS, 2.000 / 1.250 / 1.062 syscalls per I/O (every result needs its own `aio_return`), batch p50 71.8 / 89.0 / 189.2 µs against a max-of-b-QD-1-draws prediction of 72.3 / 85.6 / 124.8 µs. Batch 16 is **0.51× the 155,741 of sixteen threads**. An early hypothesis that macOS caps AIO at its `kern.aiothreads` = 4 workers was refuted by the same row (79,200 > QD-4's 52,450) and removed, not explained.

**Lane 1c — what a syscall costs.** Warm page cache, ns per 4 KiB page at 1/2/4/8/16/32/64 pages per call (`pread`, then one `preadv` over contiguous pages), best of 3: 612 / 435 / 271 / 225 / 217 / 196 / 195 — 2.82× at 16, 3.14× at 64. Two-term fit: ≈ 424 ns per call + 188 ns per page. The io_uring paper's "roughly 5–6×" at batch 16 (§2.1) is for io_uring submissions; this lane's floor includes a 4 KiB memcpy, and contiguous `preadv` is an upper bound on what syscall batching alone buys.

**Lane 2 (io_uring) has no reference timings, and the notes say why.** macOS has no io_uring. A Linux container on the same Mac (Docker Desktop, kernel `7.0.14-linuxkit`) builds the crate with `-D warnings` and runs lanes 1 and 3, but `io_uring_setup` fails with **EPERM** under Docker's default seccomp profile (the bench prints it as a stub note; running unconfined was not done), and the container's own lane 1 fails the sanity check: QD-1 p50 35.8 µs and a sequential "ceiling" of **16,872 MiB/s**, 2.8× the physical SSD — the VM disk is served from the host page cache. A reference `uring_reads` was written and compiled for Linux against `io-uring` 0.7.15, never run; the two Linux-gated tests are the contract (same bytes as `pread`; at most n/batch + qd + 1 enters).

**Lane 3 — write amplification on a simulated FTL (labelled a model everywhere).** 1,000 addressable 1 MiB erase blocks of 256 × 4 KiB pages + 100 over-provisioned, greedy GC, one stream; a 230,144-page database (90% fill minus a 256-page doublewrite buffer), 768,000 user writes, first half uncounted. In-place + doublewrite: DB 2.00 × SSD 4.13 = **8.25** uniform, 2.00 × 4.46 = **8.91** at Zipf(0.8) — deterministic, identical on Linux. Reference out-of-place implementation (stubbed before commit; numbers in notes.md): one erase-block zone 5.13 × **1.00** = 5.13 (uniform) and 6.36 × 1.00 = 6.36 (Zipf); half-block zones 5.00 × 2.00 = 10.00 / 6.18 × 2.13 = 13.16; two open zones 5.18 × 2.02 = 10.45 / 6.42 × 2.97 = 19.07; quarter-block zones, four open 4.83 × 4.43 = **21.39** / 5.97 × 4.45 = 26.54. Mean victim valid ratio 0.805 → 1/(1 − 0.805) = 5.13, the greedy identity to the digit. **The negative result is the paper's**: out-of-place writes are only a win when aligned and single-stream; misaligned or multiplexed they are worse than in-place, up to 2.6×. Test-sized device (64-page blocks, 200 + 20): in-place 2.00/3.78/7.57; aligned 4.44/1.00/4.44 (valid 0.775); half-block 4.01/2.51/10.09 — the four stub tests were run green against the reference before stubbing.

**Paper claims, each checked against the text.** Jasny et al. (VLDB'26, arXiv 2512.04859): "swapping in io_uring alone doesn't help" **checks out** — Figure 1 1.06× (libaio 173.0 → 183.5 k tx/s) and 1.10× (epoll 12.0 → 13.2 GiB/s) naive vs 2.05× (376.4/183.5, stopping at +IOPoll) and 2.31× redesigned (§1); synchronous Posix = synchronous io_uring = 16.5 k tx/s (§3.3). Models recomputed: 1/(0.7 × 82 µs) = 17.4 k vs 16.5 k; 1/(0.7 × 70 µs) = 20.4 k vs 19.1 k; 3.7×10⁹/(8,264 + 0.7 × 15,900) = 190.8 k vs 183.5 k; with 11,100 cycles 230.8 k vs 216.6 k (Table 1). "Guidelines gave ~14% in PostgreSQL" is **only partly right**: the abstract says 14%, §5.2 and §7 say 11–15%; Figure 18 times 28.35 → 24.81 s (1 worker), 14.87 → 13.00, 7.79 → 6.96, 3.97 → 3.57 compute to 14.3 / 14.4 / 11.9 / 11.2% (labels 14.29 / 14.36 / 11.86 / 11.46%). Two discrepancies recorded rather than smoothed: §3.4.1's prose step gains (+11/+20/+21/+32%) do not match its own Figure 5 bars as new/old − 1 (+9.8/+26.4/+25.3/+45.2%); and the paper's "PostgreSQL's heuristic of enabling IO_ASYNC after four outstanding I/Os" never fires for direct I/O at the pinned PostgreSQL — `pgaio_uring_should_use_async` returns false for non-buffered I/O at `method_io_uring.c:722-723` before the `> 4` check at `:729`, and the paper names no version. Table 2 batching latency 11.51 / 24.22 / 60.62 / 116.40 / 200.85 / 317.51 µs at batch 1–256.
